Source: National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration, World Meteorological Organization, Food and Agriculture Organization, Australian Bureau of Meteorology, World Bank. Data as of

October 30, 2026.
How will El Niño impact the global economy and markets? Which regions will be
most affected? El Niño’s impact on the global economy and markets is likely to be felt
most via commodity price inflation. Alongside higher energy and fertilizer costs resulting
from war in Europe and the Middle East, El Niño could put further upward pressure on
commodities like wheat, sugar, corn, soy, cocoa and coffee as drought (or extreme rain)
weighs on crop yields and production. Latest estimates from the United Nations Food

and Agriculture Organization (FAO) suggest global food prices were up 5.8% year-over-
year (YoY) in September (Exhibit 2). Livestock is also likely to be challenged by higher
temperatures globally, while flooding has already threatened copper production in
Chile. Meanwhile, with hydropower accounting for more than half of the average annual
electricity generation in Brazil, Colombia, Peru and other Latin American countries,2
below-average rainfall in affected areas has huge implications for electricity generation.

As an example, Colombia’s natural gas demand nearly doubled during the 2023-2024 El
Niño event.2 Another key watchpoint will be whether drier conditions reduce water levels
1 India Meteorological Department, Bloomberg. Refers to rainfall between June and September as of September 30, 2026.
2 International Energy Agency, as of October 7, 2026
Investment Implications
We expect El Niño to place upward
pressure on agricultural goods
prices globally. We continue to

see commodities as a strategic
diversifier and hedge against inflation
in portfolios. The U.S. remains our
preferred region with companies in
Industrials and Materials as potential
beneficiaries. Meanwhile, economic
impacts to the emerging world will vary
by country but the broader Emerging
Market (EM) equity market could be
less exposed given its significant tilt
toward Technology.

### Page 3

3 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
in the Panama Canal and other key shipping routes, particularly in light of the Strait of
Hormuz closure.
These pressures won’t be equally distributed and will heavily depend on this El Niño’s
severity. Among the more vulnerable to higher agricultural prices will be heavily import-
dependent countries in the Middle East, Africa, and parts of Asia. So too will economies
that count food as a larger share of consumer spending such as the Philippines (36%),

Indonesia (33%), Egypt (33%), India (28%), and Vietnam (26%) (Exhibit 3). Developed
economies like the U.S. (6%), Canada (10%), and Germany (11%) stand to be better
insulated from a food-related shock to headline inflation.
Within the U.S., some impacts could be positive with a potentially milder winter reducing
heating costs and wetter conditions in the South improving agricultural conditions. A recent
paper from the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco found that, in addition to putting

upward pressure on personal consumption expenditures (PCE) inflation, strong El Niño
episodes have been historically associated with a temporary boost to employment in the
following year via better construction conditions in the North and weather-event recovery
efforts.3 In California and parts of the Southwest, El Niño will be yet another stress test for
property insurers as flooding and landslide risks increase.
For more vulnerable developing economies, meanwhile, higher food and energy costs could

present a policy dilemma. On one hand, governments in vulnerable areas may move to
introduce fiscal relief to support households and boost growth. History points to plenty of
examples of this, such as Indonesia’s cash assistance in late 2023 or Thailand’s $1.8 billion
in loans to farmers in 2015. On the other hand, elevated inflation expectations and, in
some cases, weaker currencies could limit EM central banks’ room to ease monetary policy,
particularly with several major developed central banks aligned in hiking interest rates. Also

complicating matters: Global sovereign debt surpassed $110 trillion in Q2 2026, leaving
less fiscal space to absorb incoming shocks.4
How can investors position portfolios accordingly? In many ways, the prospect of a
very strong El Niño only reinforces structural trends we’ve identified as defining the coming
years: a return to higher inflation and higher interest rates; elevated commodity prices
amid heightened geopolitical risk and resource/supply shortages; and greater investment in

energy infrastructure and supply chain resilience.
Considering these themes, we continue to like commodities not just as a hedge against
higher inflation but also as a diversification tool in portfolios given their negative correlation
with Equities and bonds. That’s not to mention secular forces placing upward pressure on
commodity prices such as the resource-intensive AI buildout and broader protectionism. To
wit, the Bloomberg Commodity Index, which spans oil, gold, various metals and agricultural

goods has gained nearly 30% this year, handily outperforming the S&P 500 (+1%) and the
Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond Index (-1%).
Within Equities, the U.S. is still our preferred region. While upside risk to inflation remains
a risk to watch going into 2027, food is a relatively small share of the consumer basket in
the U.S., and neither strong services spending nor capital investment should be materially
affected by El Niño. Companies within the Industrials and Materials sectors focused on

precision agriculture, water management and crop protection may be positioned to benefit.
Import-dependent EM economies remain particularly exposed, though impacts will vary
widely depending on the magnitude and timing of weather events. It’s worth noting too that
EM Equities remain heavily concentrated in East Asian markets with substantial exposure to
technology, leaving the broader index arguably less exposed to the economic ramifications
of El Niño than in past episodes. Indeed, even after recent weakness in semiconductor

names, Taiwan, Korea, and China together account for a near-70% of the broader MSCI
Emerging Markets Index.5 We maintain a slight overweight to the region in portfolios given
earnings strength but favor an active approach.

MARKET VIEW
Another Tale of Market Rotation
Kirsten Cabacungan, Vice President and Senior Investment Strategist
October offered another reminder that headline index returns do not always tell the
full story. The S&P 500 ended the month slightly lower, down almost 1%, but the more
muted headline return masked meaningful shifts in market leadership beneath the
surface. At the center of the volatility was a sharp unwind in the AI trade, particularly

across semiconductors and memory stocks, with the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index
experiencing a 19% intramonth drawdown. The dominant concerns in recent months
have centered on the sustainability of AI infrastructure spending and whether the scale
of investment will ultimately generate sufficient returns. Those only intensified amid a
sharp rise in bond yields after the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield spiked above 5.3% as of
October 5 for the first time since 2002, raising scrutiny over AI financing conditions and

whether higher borrowing costs could increase hurdle rates for continued investment
and slow the pace of capital spending.
Yet the pressure on AI market leaders has not translated into a broad retreat from U.S.
Equities. Instead, capital has moved toward previously lagging areas of the market,
with Value-oriented, defensive and rate-sensitive stocks gaining ground. Value defeated
Growth in October, with the Russell 1000 Value Index outperforming its Growth

counterpart by 10 percentage points, an outcome seen in only about 1% of months since
1979. The move extends Value's outperformance for the year, with the index up 23%
year-to-date compared with down almost 1% for Growth. Participation also improved.
The equal-weighted S&P 500 outperformed its market-cap-weighted counterpart by its
widest monthly gap since 2009 (Exhibit 4A), while the share of S&P 500 constituents
trading above their 200-day moving average rebounded to 62% from just 43% at the

end of September, and 76% of stocks outperformed the index for the month.

not possible to invest directly in an index.

The latest rotation is not the first attempt at a change in market leadership this year.
The more important question is what that shift represents: a fundamental reassessment
of the AI trade or another step toward broader, more mature market leadership?
Recent Q3 Technology earnings results have sharpened that debate. Investors are
increasingly looking beyond the scale of AI spending to companies’ ability to translate
that investment into durable growth and attractive returns. The result may be greater

dispersion not only between Growth and Value, but also among AI leaders as investors
become more discerning about which companies can justify the scale of their
investments.
Investment Implications
We continue to look for potential
missing opportunities and favor
diversification across and within asset
classes in a multi-asset portfolio.
In particular, we see potential
opportunities to broaden exposure
beyond AI leaders toward areas
where earnings, capital spending and

productivity trends are improving,
including Value, Small- and Mid-caps,
and select non-U.S. markets, particularly
EMs and Japan. As market leadership
evolves, maintaining diversified
exposures and periodically rebalancing
portfolios can help investors participate
in a broader set of possible return
drivers without becoming overly
dependent on any single theme.

### Page 5

5 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
But greater selectivity does not necessarily signal the end of the AI investment cycle.
YoY growth rates may become harder to maintain after two years of rapid acceleration
as the comparison base rises, but absolute levels of AI capital spending remain elevated.
What matters more is whether spending translates into strong productivity and earnings
growth ahead and whether AI demand continues to support investment. October’s

volatility may therefore reflect less a retreat from the AI theme and more a recalibration
of valuations and expected returns, as other areas of the market begin to contribute
more meaningfully to market performance.
Several developments ahead will help determine whether that rotation has staying
power. One important test is whether broader participation can persist if pressure on AI
stocks continues. Continued strength outside the AI trade would support the case that

investors are reallocating capital rather than broadly reducing risk. The path of monetary
policy will also matter as investors assess where borrowing costs may be headed. The
Federal Reserve’s not-so-hawkish hold in October offered some reassurance, but the
path ahead remains dependent on the evolving balance between inflation and growth.
Any signs of resolution of geopolitical tensions remain an important swing factor
particularly as recent flashpoints in the Middle East have pressured energy prices higher

again. Results of the midterm elections should also provide greater visibility into the
policy landscape for the next few years. While the market does not need every clearing
event to resolve favorably, greater clarity around these uncertainties could help narrow
the range of potential outcomes facing investors.
For investors, October’s sudden change in leadership reinforces the importance of
diversification. Leadership can shift quickly, leaving portfolios that become overly

dependent on a single theme, sector or style particularly vulnerable when it does. We
remain constructive on U.S. Equities and believe that the market may be transitioning
more toward a mid-cycle phase in which returns become broader but also more
measured compared to the sharp acceleration earlier in the cycle. That argues not for
abandoning the market’s long-term winners, but for complementing them with exposure
to additional sources of returns across style, size, sector and region.

### Page 6

6 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
Reorienting around AI: From Corporate Plans to Construction to Capital Goods
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
For all the questions (and recent market weakness) hanging over the AI trade, from
monetization and cash burn to financing risk and overcapacity, a measurable aspect is
the buildout itself. Data center construction is lifting construction spending and capital

goods orders, while companies are adding and reviving capacity to meet AI-related
demand. Still, planning delays, access to power, labor scarcity, local politics and the cost
of capital remain meaningful constraints on the buildout’s pace or profitability.
Corporate Plans. Take Caterpillar as one example that has resumed production of a
10-megawatt natural gas turbine discontinued back in 2022. A 175-year old glassmaker,
Corning, is leaning into optical connectivity as AI clusters require faster connections of

optical fiber cabling between servers. Caterpillar and Corning are just two companies
among others pivoting to supply data centers.
Construction to Capital Goods. The AI reorientation is evident in construction
spending as well, where data center construction has outpaced every other major
residential and nonresidential category. Over the last year AI-related construction on data
centers has surged more than 60% while spending on residential, lodging, commercial

or office has declined notably (Exhibit 5A). Data center construction is running at an $85
billion (B) annualized rate this year, versus roughly $49B a year earlier, and now accounts
for 11% of private nonresidential construction, up from just 2% in 2022 according to
the Census Bureau. Similarly, demand for artificial intelligence business equipment and
transportation equipment drove an increase in durable goods orders in September on the
back of new orders for U.S.-manufactured capital goods, suggesting business spending

on equipment remained robust in Q3. Another factor, see Exhibit 5B, where critical
components such as generators, switchboards, chillers and transformers continue to
face long lead times that affect delivery timelines and ultimate profitability for new data
center development.
These signposts reinforce our view that we are only in the middle innings of the AI
investment cycle. From an investment perspective, huge demand for data centers, power
systems, networking infrastructure, specialized chips and other equipment continues

to strain supply, creating both bottlenecks and potential opportunities for companies
powering the buildout.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500/Market-cap Weighted Index is a market-capitalization-weighted index that is widely regarded as the best single gauge of large-cap U.S. equities. The index includes 500 leading

companies and covers approximately 80% of available market capitalization.
S&P 500 Equal-Weighted Index includes the same constituents as the capitalization weighted S&P 500, but each company in the S&P 500 EWI is allocated a fixed weight - or 0.2% of the
index total at each quarterly rebalance.
Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond Index is a broad-based, market-capitalization-weighted benchmark that measures the performance of the U.S. investment-grade, U.S. dollar-denominated,

fixed-rate taxable bond market
Bloomberg Commodity Index is a broadly diversified financial benchmark that tracks the price movements of physical commodities through futures contracts.
MSCI Emerging Market Index captures large and mid cap representation across 24 Emerging Markets (EM) countries.
United Nations Food and Agriculture Food Price Index is a measure of the monthly change in international prices of a basket of food commodities.
Philadelphia Semiconductor Index is a market-cap-weighted index focusing on 30 leading semiconductor firms, offering insights into the tech market.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

STRONG RESILIENCE TO EXTERNAL SHOCKS
, 
SOLID 
MACROECONOMIC FUNDAMENTALS 
◼ 
 Over the past 
 two years
 , India has demonstrated remarkable resilience in the face of external shocks, while its 
macroeconomic fundamentals have remained solid.  
◼ 
 In FY2025/2026  
(ended in March 2026)
 , India was hit by a sharp rise in US tariffs. However, thanks to supportive fiscal 
and monetary policies, 
 real GDP  
grew by 7.8% in FY2026, placing India among the fastest
 -
 growing Asian economies.  
◼ 
 For the current fiscal year, the Indian economy is facing rising energy prices 
 (
due to 
 the conflict in the Middle East and 
increased food prices 
 because 
 of 
an 
unfavourable monsoon
 ) 
as well as 
 monetary tightening. Nevertheless, despite these 
new challenges, real GDP growth is expected to exceed 7%.  
◼ 
 India is currently in a stronger position than it was in 2022 to cope with the new energy shock. The fiscal leeway to support  
the economy has increased with the decline in the fiscal deficit, and inflation is more contained.  
◼ 
 Although 
 rising  
energy prices have put pressure on external accounts, FX reserves 
 remain  
sufficient to cover the country’s 
short
 -
 term external financing needs. 
 Yet, 
downward pressures on the rupee have been 
 significant 
 and 
could persist in the 
short term
 . 
In 
H1 
2026, India’s external accounts withstood the rise in energy prices fairly well
 , notably  
thanks to the sharp increase 
in workers’ remittances. 
 T
he 
widening of the trade deficit was primarily due to the substantial increase in machinery and 
equipment imports 
 while t
 he oil trade deficit increased 
 only 
moderately, as the rise in energy prices was offset by a decline 
in import volumes. 
 However, 
in H2 2026, the oil trade deficit is expected to widen significantly.  
Additionally, large portfolio outflows have exacerbated downward pressure on the rupee, necessitating interventions by 
the central bank in foreign exchange markets and the adoption of regulatory measures to support the currency. Although 
these measures hav
 e led to a rebound in the rupee (at least temporarily), inflationary pressures, volatile portfolio 
investments, and low net foreign direct investments are 
 expected to continue 
 to put pressure on the currency.  
◼ 
 D
ownward pressures on the rupee 
 and 
 rising bond yields will reduce the government's 
 already limited 
 fiscal capacity to 
increase public investment and 
 support economic development. However, this will not undermine its ability to refinance 
its debt, given its safe structure.  
◼ 
 T
he banking sector is sufficiently robust to cope with the expected rise in credit risks.

STRONG ECONOMIC GROWTH DESPITE 
STRUCTURAL CONSTRAINTS 
◼ 
 In October 2026, the IMF revised upward 
 India’s  
medium
 -
 term growth 
 forecast 
 from 6.5% to 6.8%  
per year
 . Nonetheless, 
to achieve such strong growth performance, 
 India will have to adapt to the challenges posed by artificial intelligence  
(AI)
, 
while its infrastructure and education system are not prepared for it.  
◼ 
 Moreover, India’s development remains highly uneven. 
 The middle class is still modest
 . This is explained by a large 
informal economy, heavy concentration of employment in low value
 -
 added sectors such as agriculture, low participation 
rates among women and youth, and limited industrial development. All this, in turn, reflects India’s weak i
 ntegration into 
global value chains.  
◼ 
 India faces two key structural challenges: i
 ) adapting the services sector to AI and ii) accelerating industrial development. 
Even with progress in sectors such as smartphones and pharmaceuticals, India’s industrial base remains too small to 
absorb its vast labour force. 
 Labo
u
r market 
 rigidities  
and the mismatch between training and business needs are holding 
back both the creation of quality jobs and industrial development.  
◼ 
 Low net foreign direct investment is a 
 major  
 obstacle. While 
 India attracts 
 new 
 FDI, retention is weak
 : 
 despite 
improvements in logistics, governance issues and regulatory barriers have 
 led 
 disinvestments to triple over the past five 
years.  
◼ 
 India 
 may fail in completing its 
 transition to a higher
 -
 value, more industrialised and inclusive economy if reforms do not 
accelerate 
 significantly
 . Structural change
 s 
could lag behind pressures from its rapidly growing population and 
 fast 
global 
technological advances

INDIA
’S COMPLEX POSITION 
IN INTERNATIONAL TRADE 
Low 
attractiveness  
due to unfavourable business environment and limited integration in global value chains  
◼ 
 Despite very strong growth, India's attractiveness is low relative to the size of its economy. The stock of foreign direct 
investment stood at only 15% of GDP in Q2 2026 compared to 52% of GDP in Vietnam and around 67% of GDP in Thailand 
and Malaysia.  
◼ 
 This lack of appeal can be attributed to 
 India’s still unfavo
 u
rable 
 business environment and 
 its 
 limited integration into 
global value chains. India’s global market share remain
 s 
 low (1.9% of total 
 world 
 exports  
 of goods
 )
, 
 especially in 
comparison with that of China (16.1% of total exports).  
◼ 
 India has not benefited from the AI boom
 , 
since its exports of AI
 -
 related goods remain modest (0.5% of its total exports)
 . 
However, 
 it has benefited from the “China +1” strategy adopted by 
 international 
 companies seeking to diversify their 
production sources. Smartphone exports by India have increased 6.3
 -
 fold over the past five years and accounted for almost 
6% of global exports in 2025 compared to less than 1% five year
 s 
earlier.  
To 
increase its a
 ttractiveness and develop its industrial sector, India has 
 multiplied F
 ree 
T
rade 
 A
greements  
◼ 
 Over the past 18 months, 
 India  
has signed a growing number of free trade agreements 
 – 
with the European Free Trade 
Association in October 2025, the United Kingdom in July 2025, Oman in December 2025, and the European Union in January 
2026
. This 
 should help to develop the industrial base and demonstrates the Modi government's commitment to 
accelerating India's integration into global trade.  
The sectors most likely to benefit from the recent trade agreement with the EU are electronics and semiconductors, 
pharmaceuticals and chemical
 s
, textile and renewable energy technologies. But expanding trade agreements without deep 
domestic improvements in the business environment, governance, education, and infrastructure will be ineffective. India 
will continue to struggle with investment reten
 tion. 
◼ 
 The US
 -
 India bilateral trade agreement has not yet been concluded. The two sides reached a framework for an interim 
Agreement in February 2026, under which the US would apply an 18% reciprocal tariff on Indian goods, with further 
reductions subject to the 
 successful conclusion of the agreement. As of early October, negotiations 
 are 
stalled. Key sticking 
points include market access, particularly 
 in 
India’s sensitive agricultural sector.

COUNTRY RISK PROFILE

Non transfer risk   
India’s external accounts are solid with a low external debt, moderate current account deficit and large FX 
reserves. 
 India’s net debtor position reached only 5.9% of GDP in Q2 2026. In the short term, external accounts 
are expected to deteriorate 
 given 
the rise in energy prices while downward pressures on the rupee should remain 
high.  
◼ 
 External accounts have remained solid 
 despite recent adverse shocks
 . 
 In 2025, external accounts remained solid 
despite the significant increase in US tariffs. In 
 H1 
 2026, they held up well against rising energy prices. Although the 
current account deficit is expected to widen for the current fiscal year as a whole, it should remain contained, and foreign 
exchange reserves should be more than sufficient to cover the co
 untry’s short
 -
 term financing needs. The 
 major  
causes 
for concern are the 
 structurally 
 low level of foreign direct investment (although it is rising) and the increased reliance on 
portfolio investment, which exposes the currency to greater volatility.  
◼ 
 Structural vulnerability to energy price shock
 . 
India is structurally vulnerable to an energy price shock as its 
 oil and gas  
trade deficit is relatively high (2.8% of GDP in 2025). However, in H1 2026, the current account withstood fairly well the 
rise in energy prices especially thanks to the sharp increase in workers’ remittances  
(+0.7 pp to 3.7% of GDP)
 . The trade 
deficit widened significantly but principally because of the strong increase in machinery and equipment imports rather

◼ 
 In FY2026 (ended in March 2026), public finances continued to improve. 
 The central government’s fiscal deficit 
narrowed to 4.4% of GDP from 5% of GDP the previous year. 
 However, t
 his reduction stemmed primarily from a significant

at

37.6% of total government 
revenue
s

The fiscal position is stronger than during the 2022 energy crisis, with the general government deficit estimated 
at 
7.8% of GDP  
in FY2026, compared to  
9.5% 
in FY2021/2022.  
◼ 
 For FY2027, the initial budget targets a slight reduction in the central 
 government 
 deficit, to 4.3% of GDP. This goal may 
however 
 prove challenging due to rising subsidies amid the energy shock.  
To cushion households from the shock, the government initially 
 kept 
petrol prices 
 unchanged
 . Refining firms absorbed the 
financial burden, partially offset by lower excise duties on petroleum products. Only after regional elections in mid

 May 
did the government allow a modest increase in pump prices to reflect higher international 
 prices
 . 
The government has 
also increased subsidies  
to offset the impact of rising fertiliser prices on farmers.  
Revenue losses  
—

could reach  
0.5% of GDP
 , while 
the cost of 
 fertiliser subsidies 
 could 
 rise from  
0.4% to 0.8% of GDP.  
To reduce the risk of fiscal slippage, the government 
has: i) raised taxes on fuel exports, ii) capped household diesel purchases; and iii) planned cuts to certain expenditures.

 While the primary deficit reduction

 is 
another source of severe concern
 .

is 
expected due to

 . This is expected to 
 increase the debt

 gradually

Systemic credit risk  
The banking sector has strengthened over the past five years
 , 
as illustrated by the improvement in 
 asset  
quality 
and capital buffers. The main sources of credit risks are rising household debt, the rise in energy prices, the 
unfavourable monsoon and monetary tightening.  
◼ 
 The quality of assets has improved significantly. 
 In Q1 2026, the banking sector’s non
 -
 performing loan (NPL) ratio fell 
to a multi
 -
 decade low of 1.8%. For public banks  
(
54.4% of total loans
 )
, the NPL ratio declined to 2.1%.  
◼ 
 Capital buffers are strong. 
 While 
loan loss provisions remained modest at 75.6% of NPLs, capital buffers stayed strong. 
The Capital Adequacy Ratio (CAR) rose to 17.7% in Q1 2026, up from 17.4% a year earlier. From April 2027, Indian banks 
will adopt a new Expected Credit Loss (ECL) framewo
 rk, requiring earlier provisioning for potential loan losses. In the 
short term, higher provisioning requirements could push up lending rates and tighten credit conditions 
 while 
 triggering 
capital raising or targeted government recapitalisation. 
 I
n the medium term, the reform could strengthen banking  
sector 
resilience through earlier recognition of credit risks and more robust loss buffers.  
◼ 
 Banks’ 
 liquidity 
 is 
 comfortable
 . 
It deteriorated slightly in H1 2026 because credit growth outpaced deposit growth
 ; t
he 
liquidity coverage ratio decli
 ned to 
a still solid 
 124% 
and 
the loan
 -
 to
-
 deposit ratio increased to 83% in Q2 2026 from 79.1% 
a year earlier. RBI interventions to support the rupee 
 also 
 drained liquidity
 . Moreover
 , the deposit mix shifted from low
 -
cost current and savings accounts (CASA) to higher
 -
 cost term deposits, pressuring net interest margins. However, since 
June 2026, liquidity has improved significantly due to RBI measures to attract foreign
 -
 currency non
 -
 re
sident (FCNR) 
deposits.  
◼ 
 Profitability remains satisfactory. 
 It 
 has declined since the start of the year, primarily due to narrowing net interest 
margins, but remains satisfactory. In Q2 2026, 
 the 
 return on assets (ROA) and return on equity (ROE) stood at 1.6% and 
13.0%, respectively (IMF data).  
◼ 
 P
rivate
 -
 sector  
 debt 
 is moderate
 , 
 but its increase 
 needs to be monitored.  
 It stood 
 at 105.1% of GDP in Q1 2026 (BIS  
data
), compared to 100.1% five years earlier
 , which is 
 lower than in other emerging Asian economies.  
Non
-
 financial corporat
 e 
debt was 56% of GDP in Q1 2026. Listed firms’ financial positions continued to strengthen amid 
surging profits, improving their ability to service debt. In Q2 2026, the 
 average 
 interest coverage ratio (ICR) rose to 7.4, up 
from 6.1 a year earlier. However, the share of companies with an ICR below 1 (insufficient earnings to cover interest costs) 
increased to 18.5%. Stress remain
 s 
 concentrated in micro
 -
 enterprises, agriculture, and energy
 -
 intensive sectors (
 e.g.
, 
power, steel, cement, aviation), which are 
 the 
most exposed to oil
 -
 price shocks.  
Household leverage requires monitoring. Although still moderate, household debt increased to 49.1% of GDP in Q1 2026, 
up from 42.7% five years earlier, driven largely by non
 -
 housing retail loans (58.4% of total borrowing).  
◼ 
 Monetary 
 policy 
 tightening. 
 Due to rising  
inflation (+5.5% year
 -
 on
-
 year in September) and downward pressure on the 
rupee, the RBI raised its policy rate by 25 basis points to 5.5% in October 2026 under a "calibrated tightening" stance.  
◼ 
 T
he banking sector should remain resilient  
 despite rising risks in the short term. 
 Due to rising energy prices, a poor 
monsoon, and monetary tightening, asset quality is expected to deteriorate, particularly for low
 -
 income borrowers, micro, 
and small enterprises. 
 E
arly warning signs are emerging
 , with some 
 borrowers starting to miss payments. The stress is 
concentrated in unsecured retail loans  
(
which account for over half of fresh retail slippages
 )
. 
This could lead to new 
 NPLs 
over the coming quarters, especially as higher rates and inflation squeeze repayment capacity. Additionally, in the near 
term, capital inflows may remain weak, 
 and 
 liquidity and funding pressures could intensify.

Document 373: Global Economics Analyst_ Macro Outlook 2027_ Steady Baseline, Downside Risks

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Economics Analyst_ Macro Outlook 2027_ Steady Baseline, Downside Risks.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 30
Status: ok

### Page 2

Macro Outlook 2027: Steady Baseline, Downside Risks

We entered 2026 expecting sturdy global growth and slower inflation. The war in Iran 
derailed this inflation forecast, but the global economy proved more resilient than 
feared at the start of the war. 
Even though global headline inflation is on course to end 2026 at 3.7% on a Q4/Q4 
basis—1.3pp higher than our forecast from last year—we estimate that the global 
economy still grew at a remarkably unremarkable 2.7%, in line with potential and only 
0.1pp below our projections from our 2026 outlook (Exhibit 1). Our interpretation is that 
surging AI capex, easier financial conditions, and (in the US, Germany, and parts of Asia) 
fiscal expansion mostly offset the energy supply shock. As a result of these tailwinds, 
GDP growth only modestly underperformed in the US (-0.3pp), Euro area (-0.3pp), and 
China (-0.2pp) while significantly outperforming in Asian economies such as Taiwan 
(+8.6pp) and Korea (+1.9pp) that benefited from surging AI exports.

Growth Remains Solid but Unremarkable in 2027 
We expect another year of solid but unremarkable GDP growth in 2027, but several of 
the impulses that shaped global growth in 2026 will look different. 
The headwind from the energy supply shock will likely flip next year as energy prices 
normalize. Our commodity strategists’ baseline is that continued Middle East supply 
adaptation will lower Brent prices to $78/bbl by end-2027 with a similar normalization 
in global LNG prices (Exhibit 2). Based on these projections, we forecast that the energy 
price impulse to global growth will swing from a 0.4pp drag on global growth in 2026 to 
a modest boost in 2027 (although we see a continued drag in Europe from delayed 
energy cost pass-through to consumers).

Exhibit 1: Inflation Surged, but Growth Proved Resilient in 2026

### Page 3

A positive growth impulse from energy prices should outweigh the effect of tighter 
financial conditions and a smaller fiscal impulse in 2027. Our GS Financial Conditions 
Index (GS FCI) has tightened by around 50bp in the US and by 30bp in developed 
markets (DMs) over the last three months on the back of lower equity prices and higher 
rates. It now implies a moderate drag on growth over the next few quarters (Exhibit 3). 
We do not expect meaningful fiscal consolidation owing to higher rates in 2027, but the 
large fiscal expansions of the last few years are fading. The main exception is Japan, 
where consumption tax cuts, an increase in defense spending, and the Takaichi 
administration’s investment plans point to a meaningful 0.7pp boost to GDP growth. We 
also expect modestly positive impulses in Germany and the US—where the Democratic 
victory in November’s US election suggests some of the spending cuts from the 2025 
One Big Beautiful Bill Act will be reversed—but a continued fiscal drag in the UK that 
leaves the DM fiscal impulse only slightly positive.

Exhibit 2: Oil Prices Are On Course to Partially Normalize, Which Will Turn a Large Headwind into a Modest Tailwind

### Page 4

The main global growth impulse that we do not expect to change in 2027 is increased AI 
capex. We forecast that nominal global AI investment will rise from $1.1tn in 2026 to 
$1.8tn in 2027, mostly driven by an increase of over $500bn in hardware spending in the 
US (Exhibit 4). We expect the investment surge to be frontloaded, with the 
year-over-year change in investment as a share of GDP peaking in 2027H1 (the level of 
investment as a share of GDP peaks in 2028 under our forecast). Even with the expected 
slowdown, the impact on GDP growth will be sizable: we estimate that AI investment will 
boost GDP growth by 0.4pp globally. And although we again estimate only a small 0.2pp 
boost to measured US GDP growth in 2027 owing to continued undercounting of 
semiconductor investment and chip-related intellectual property exports, we estimate a 
larger 0.6pp boost to “true” GDP growth after correcting for these measurement biases.

Exhibit 3: The Combined Effects of Fiscal Policy and Financial Conditions Are Poised to Turn Roughly Neutral in 2027

### Page 5

The other notable growth impulse from 2026 that will continue in 2027 is a drag on 
rest-of-the-world growth from China. Our forecasts for China remain above consensus 
for 2027 (4.5% GS vs. 4.4% consensus), but this again reflects very robust expectations 
for Chinese exports and a further increase in China’s trade surplus (Exhibit 5). By 
contrast, we expect domestic demand growth to remain weak in 2027 owing to a 1.3pp 
of GDP property sector drag and weak underlying consumer spending, resulting in a 
modest 0.1pp headwind to GDP growth for the rest of the world, with larger growth 
drags in manufacturing-heavy economies such as Germany (-0.3pp) and Central Europe 
(-0.2pp).

### Page 6

Taken together, our 2027 global GDP growth forecast is modestly optimistic at 2.8%, a 
pace that is 0.1pp above our estimate of potential. As has been the case for the last 
several years, we are more optimistic on US growth (2.4% vs. 2.2% consensus) owing to 
resilient investment and more downbeat on the European growth outlook (0.9% vs. 
1.3%) owing to tighter financial conditions and our expectation that higher energy 
prices will start to bite this winter. We also expect continued robust growth in South 
Korea (3.2% vs. 2.7%) and Taiwan (6.3% vs. 5.9%) reflecting another year of strong AI 
exports.

Downside Risks 
We see risks around our baseline global growth forecasts as skewed to the downside for 
three reasons. 
1. A Renewed Energy Shock 
The first and most immediate risk is that an escalation of the war in Iran could lead to 
more significant energy price increases, creating growth headwinds that—unlike in 
2026—FCI and fiscal impulses would not offset in 2027. While our commodity strategists 
see risks around their energy price baselines as two-sided, they put more weight on an 
upside scenario where intensified shipping attacks, damage to oil infrastructure, and a 
more persistent risk premium push oil prices up to $132/bbl (Exhibit 7). If these risks 
materialize, the energy price impulse would remain a moderate drag on global GDP 
growth in 2027.

2. Bond Market Turbulence 
Second, we worry about a bigger fiscal risk premium. In 2026, we saw the consequences 
of large-scale deficits—coupled with AI-linked borrowing and central bank hikes—show 
up more clearly in higher longer-term interest rates. Although rates have eased 
somewhat over the last few weeks, the DM yield curve remains 85bp above its level in 
early 2026. If sustained, we estimate that this increase would subtract a cumulative 0.5% 
from GDP via weakness in housing, consumer durables, and business investment (Exhibit 
8). Only part of this drag is incorporated in our standing forecasts because we expect 
bond yields to pull back as central banks deliver fewer hikes than priced in markets.

Exhibit 7: A Further Rise in Oil Prices Owing to Middle East Escalation Could Derail the Growth Outlook

These risks are especially acute in France, where concerns around fiscal sustainability 
have pushed the 10-year OAT yield about 150bp above its German counterpart (Exhibit

forecast a 0.8pp reduction in the government deficit in 2028). But fiscal health 
indicators are flashing red, raising the risk that the market could force an earlier 
consolidation and larger drag on growth.

### Page 9

3. A Weaker AI Impulse
The transition to an AI-enabled economy is occurring in two phases. The first phase
involves an investment boom that raises both corporate profits and global GDP growth
but features only limited productivity gains and labor market disruption (because it
takes firms several years to adopt AI and reorganize to take full advantage of the new
technology). The second phase involves lower hardware investment but more visible
business reorganization, as well as more substantial productivity gains and labor market

disruption.
To illustrate this transition, we overlay a simulated AI capex path with our estimates of
the labor market impacts under a 10-year adoption timeline (Exhibit 10). The capex
path assumes that US AI investment follows equity analysts’ projections through 2032,
after which the capital stock converges toward a long-run equilibrium level.1  Under our
baseline calibration, the AI capital stock eventually converges to a long-run level of
around 9% of GDP, and 9% of total US workers are displaced. (We continue to expect

most of these displaced workers to find new jobs, including in new occupations that are
created by AI).
Under these assumptions, the transition from the first phase to the second phase
doesn’t occur until 2028-2029, when we see a further pickup in the underlying US
productivity trend to the 2½-3% range and a gradual increase in the US unemployment
rate to 4½%. A backloaded transition is consistent with the projections by equity
analysts that AI investment will keep growing through 2028, albeit at a slower pace than

in 2026-2027. It is also consistent with the renewed pickup in US employment growth
since the spring, which has temporarily laid to rest concerns around imminent AI-related
job market disruption.
1 We characterize the long-run equilibrium level following Hall and Jorgenson (1967) as a function of the
depreciation rate, technological cost disinflation, nominal interest rates, and the AI capital factor share of total
output.
12 November 2026   9
Goldman Sachs Global Economics Analyst

### Page 10

But it is hard to be sure about the timing of this shift and we see some signs that it could 
occur earlier.  Hyperscaler equity price reactions to earnings call signals for higher capex 
switched from positive in 2024-2025 to slightly negative in 2026 as the market has 
become concerned about overinvestment (Exhibit 11). Furthermore, upward revisions to 
hyperscaler capex slowed in the Q3 earnings season, which suggests that the AI 
investment cycle peak is shifting more clearly into view.

### Page 11

Beyond the fundamental demand for compute, we see three other near-term downside 
risks for AI investment: 
Capacity constraints – Semiconductor and memory producers are capacity n
constrained with order backlogs through 2028. While capacity investments currently 
underway should eventually alleviate these constraints, a surge in nominal 
investment in 2027 may increasingly raise prices (thereby pushing central banks 
toward more hawkish policy) without boosting real output. 
Credit markets – Global AI-related debt issuance has totaled $687bn so far in 2026 n
while AI-related equity issuance has added another $115bn. While our market 
strategists see adequate capital supply to meet even higher capital demand next 
year, upward pressure on borrowing costs could lead to more meaningful crowd-out 
of other investment in 2027 (vs. only modest crowd-out in 2026). 
Regulation – Concerns regarding regulatory headwinds to investment surged after n
the US midterm elections in early November raised the possibility of data center 
permitting moratoria in several states (particularly in Texas). We do not anticipate 
that regulation will derail 2027 investment, both because moratoria will likely be 
short-lived and because permits for 2027 projects have mostly already been issued. 
But a more extended delay or stringent regulatory shift would raise downside risks to 
AI investment in 2028-2029, which could weigh on growth in 2027 via tighter 
financial conditions. 
Turning to the labor market, we have argued that AI adoption is likely to displace jobs 
and cause a temporary increase in the unemployment rate of around ½ a percentage 
point over the next several years.2  Our baseline expectation is that this increase occurs 
in a backloaded fashion, with only a small effect in 2027 and more notable effects in 
2028-2029.  However, we are starting to see signs that the AI impact is spreading 
2  See “Revisiting the Extent and Timing of AI Labor Displacement,” Global Economics Analyst, October 22, 
2026.

beyond technology to the broader economy. Across nonmanufacturing industries, the 
share of US firms that have adopted AI in regular business functions now shows a 
statistically significant negative correlation with employment growth and a statistically 
significant positive correlation with unemployment (correlations were insignificant at the 
start of the year, Exhibit 12).

Moreover, AI-driven job displacement would likely accelerate in a downside growth 
scenario. Exhibit 13 shows that the trend decline in the employment share of routine 
occupations over the past five decades has been highly concentrated in recessions.  
Given that AI has sharply increased the number of occupations exposed to automation, 
we would expect corporate leaders to respond to a slowdown by reducing headcount 
targets more aggressively, putting more upward pressure on the unemployment rate.

### Page 13

To be clear, we still expect AI-related pressure on the labor market to remain temporary.  
Employment in existing industries such as healthcare is likely to keep growing rapidly, 
and history shows that technological revolutions ultimately result in the creation of new 
industries and occupations that absorb workers displaced elsewhere.  Historically, this 
dynamism has kept the unemployment rate from increasing permanently despite all the 
labor-saving technological innovations of the past 250 years.  But over the next few 
years, job market disruption is likely to grow.  
Inflation: Progress Not Perfection 
Inflation progress was slower than expected in 2026 owing to price increases associated 
with the war in Iran.  But under our baseline commodity forecasts for a steady decline in 
energy prices next year, inflation should slow in 2027. 
The case for disinflation is particularly strong in the US because the price boosts from 
tariffs (which are still adding 40bp to year-over-year core PCE inflation through 
October) should fully drop out of the year-over-year inflation statistics next spring 
(Exhibit 14). Combined with a much smaller inflation boost from the war in Iran, we 
expect core PCE to slow from 3.2% at end-2026 to 2.4% at end-2027.

Exhibit 13: Prior Automation Cycles Suggest That Aggregate Automation Speeds Up in 
Recessions

### Page 14

Despite our expectation for progress, we see several reasons why inflation may remain 
somewhat firm in early 2027, thereby delaying a return to target-consistent levels in 
most economies. 
First, while most of the price boost from higher energy prices has been realized, some 
pass-through will spill into next year (Exhibit 15).  This is true both in the US and 
especially in Europe, where indirect effects have so far been very limited.  For example, 
European airfares have not repriced higher in response to fuel cost increases so far—
reflecting fuel hedges, greater competition, and pre-war plans for supply 
expansion—but should pick up notably next year.

### Page 15

Second, global memory price forecasts were again revised higher over the last earnings 
season on the back of robust demand (Exhibit 16).  This pattern both suggests more 
near-term upside to core inflation from AI spillovers—although we still anticipate price 
increases should slow as production capacity increases—and points to upside risks if 
recent pricing trends continue.  This is especially true in the US, where the impact from 
higher memory prices is exaggerated because the weight of the software and 
accessories category is overstated.

Third, upstream cost pressures have increased and broadened (Exhibit 17). These 
increases are accounted for in our baseline forecasts, but historical patterns suggest that 
the recent rise in upstream cost pressures will keep inflation surprise risks elevated 
through the spring. We are particularly concerned about a reacceleration in the US, 
where the “January effect” has driven a seasonal inflation pickup almost every year since 
the pandemic (our forecast incorporates a 0.1pp boost to January core PCE).

We still see most of these price pressures as backward-looking and temporary, however, 
and remain less worried about a sustained inflation overshoot.  We take particular 
comfort in the downward trend in wage growth in most major economies that lowers the 
risk of a pickup in core services inflation (Exhibit 18).  Furthermore, leading indicators 
continue to point toward an incremental softening in US shelter inflation, and we have 
yet to see signs of second-round effects in Europe.

Taken together, we forecast a steady decline in year-over-year core inflation in the US 
and a more delayed decline in other major economies once the energy price shock is 
fully digested (Exhibit 19).  And although core inflation remains somewhat above target 
at end-2027 on a year-over-year basis, we expect that the inflation constraint will

The largest risk to our near-term inflation view is that any renewed escalation in Iran 
could prolong headline (and to some degree core) inflation pressures.  Applying our 
standard rules of thumb to our commodity team’s adverse scenario implies 0.9pp upside 
to headline inflation and 0.2pp upside to core inflation relative to our baseline forecasts 
for 2027. 
Still Expecting Fewer Hikes Than Priced 
We forecast incremental policy tightening through early 2027 in most major economies 
(and see a low bar for modest incremental tightening in others), but our economic 
forecasts support a dovish view on rates in 2027: central banks could either deliver 
gradual normalization cuts later in the year as inflation comes down or pivot to cuts 
sooner if the downside growth risks discussed above materialize. 
In the US, we expect the Fed to deliver rate hikes in December and March in response to 
lingering inflation pressures (Exhibit 20).  But softer inflation data should allow the Fed 
to cut once in December 2027, followed by three more cuts in 2028 to a terminal rate of 
3.25-3.5%.  Furthermore, we see more dovish rate risks in the US than in most other 
economies, both because US inflation drivers look more clearly temporary and because 
the US is most exposed to a downturn in the AI cycle.

### Page 18

Our views on other major DMs also skew dovish (Exhibit 21). We expect the ECB to 
deliver its final hike in December, before starting to normalize back toward a 2.25% 
terminal rate in 2027Q4. And although higher-than-expected inflation could prompt 
another hike in Q1, our softer European growth outlook limits policy rate upside. In the 
UK, we expect a final BoE hike to 4.25% in February before a hold for the remainder of 
2027. We likewise expect normalization cuts from the BoE to a 3.25% terminal rate, but 
not until 2028. Our ECB and BoE forecasts are dovish relative to market pricing on both a 
baseline and a risk-adjusted basis. 
We are also dovish on Canada and Australia. In Canada, we expect two hikes to 3.0% in 
the first half of next year based on recent BoC communication suggesting it plans to 
return to the center of its neutral range (which we believe will be revised up to 2.5-3.5% 
in April).  But with trade uncertainty looming large, it is more likely that the BoC 
underdelivers than hikes above the midpoint of its neutral range (as the market is 
pricing).  In Australia, our RBA forecast for a hold at 4.85% through October is broadly in 
line with market pricing over 2027H1, but more dovish in 2027H2 and especially by 
end-2028 (by which time we expect a cumulative 125bp in cuts to have been delivered).  
The main outlier to our forecast of only modest further policy tightening is Japan. We 
expect the BoJ to deliver another 25bp hike at the December MPM, reflecting recent 
BoJ communication highlighting both upside inflation risks and the impact of higher 
global interest rates on the Yen. But with core CPI (ex fresh food but including energy) 
likely to rise above 3.5% through next summer, we now expect that the BoJ will follow 
through with additional hikes in March and June for a 2.0% terminal rate.

Our views also skew dovish in EMs (Exhibit 22). This is particularly true in CEEMEA and 
LatAm, where we expect most central banks to cut through 2027.  In Asia, we expect 
rates to stay flat or rise slightly (though our forecasts are still below market pricing 
except in Taiwan). Energy prices are the main upside risk to EM rates, but we think 
markets overestimate the inflation risk in most EMs for three reasons. First, inflation was 
already low before the energy shock. Second, EM economies are cyclically weaker than 
DMs. Third, EM currencies have held up well, supported by healthy external balances. 
Some EMs may tighten further over the next few quarters, but we expect most to hold or 
cut after that.

### Page 20

More Challenges for Markets than the Macro
As with the economic outlook, the market outlook continues to be dominated by the
path of the AI cycle and energy risks related to the Iran war.
Our baseline forecast is quite friendly. The mix of steady growth, falling core inflation,
gradually moderating energy prices and an earlier peak in G10 hiking cycles than market
pricing does not signal obvious threats to asset markets. The clearest difference with

market pricing is our more benign view of the policy rate path for many economies,
which all else equal should prove a boost to both equities and bonds. Our central asset
forecasts look for positive returns across the major asset classes. After the recent
sell-off, our forecasts imply robust equity returns between now and the end of 2027, but
we expect the pace of gains to slow as we move through next year.
Despite this baseline, we think the market outlook will continue to prove challenging, as

it has been already for several months. In part, this is because of the risks around our
baseline that we have already described. In part, it is because we think markets—
especially around the AI theme—have been priced in ways that leave them more
vulnerable to signs that important tailwinds may be starting to fade. In the process, the
volatility and rotations that have mostly remained under the surface of major equity and
credit markets this year could spill over more into shifts at the index level.

Managing the Peak in the AI Investment Cycle
The biggest uncertainties—as with the economic outlook—lie around the AI cycle. With
AI investment growth expected to remain robust, the peak in the AI investment impulse
should be a manageable event for the economy. But it is already proving to be a bigger
test for markets for three reasons. First, the AI impulse has been a much larger
contributor to growth in earnings, market value and capital spending than to growth in

GDP. Second, AI-related equities have taken credit for significant macro productivity
gains, especially companies benefiting directly from the infrastructure boom, that have
made them more vulnerable to any signs that we are on a less optimistic path. Third, the
point at which growth rates peak (a “second derivative” turning point) often matters,
even if underlying growth rates (the “first derivative”) remain healthy. Moving through
that peak allows markets to place limits on the upside of an uncertain distribution and as

growth decelerates, the period of positive surprises to expectations often ends too, as
we may be starting to see for the AI capex cycle.
12 November 2026   20
Goldman Sachs Global Economics Analyst

### Page 21

Markets have already been reflecting these concerns. Since June, we have seen a 
significant derating of companies levered to the AI infrastructure theme, which are most 
sensitive to shifts in the investment outlook and had been the biggest market 
beneficiaries in the first half of 2026. The good news is that robust profits, and a rising 
share of income going to listed companies, have helped keep both the imbalances and 
the deep valuation excesses that we saw in the late 1990s at bay. The key question in this 
cycle is whether the market is correctly assessing how much of the recent acceleration in 
profits, which has taken S&P 500 earnings to a record high relative to GDP, will prove 
persistent especially as the investment cycle itself fades. Without the capital inflows 
from the EM crises in 1997 and 1998—or the rate cuts that goods price deflation 
allowed—increased financing needs have also been putting clearer upward pressure on 
the cost of capital.

### Page 22

A Flatter Trend, but Risks of a Bumpier Transition 
Two things give us comfort in terms of how bumpy this transition will prove to be. The 
first is that although the investment impulse from AI is poised to peak, growth in both AI 
investment and earnings should remain firmly positive in 2027, especially earlier in the 
year. We expect S&P 500 earnings growth to decelerate from 36% in 2026 to a 
still-robust 11% in 2027. This may help to limit the de-rating of even the most directly 
affected areas and should reduce the impact on broader growth-sensitive assets. This is 
particularly true since recent anxiety about AI-related returns has pushed the valuations 
of many technology companies lower. The second is that the baton may be passed from 
the direct beneficiaries of the AI investment boom to other areas of the market. This 
includes other areas of the AI complex, such as the hyperscalers, if monetization paths 
become more visible; companies which may begin to show benefits from AI productivity 
enhancements; or non-AI companies which see some relief as the crowding out of 
capital available to them eases (and are now outperforming the broad AI complex in 
2026). 
The risk is that what we expect to be an eventual flattening in the upward trend in asset 
markets—and a rotation away from some of the largest recent winners—becomes a 
bigger aggregate shock. This could happen because the market begins to look further 
forward to the peak in the investment share itself (in 2028 on our forecasts); because 
the passing of the baton to other sectors does not take place as quickly or as smoothly 
as needed; because a fresh energy spike puts non-AI areas back under pressure; or 
because feedback loops from financial and credit conditions accelerate more quickly 
than in our baseline. We think the risk to markets is higher than the risk to the economic 
picture, because the boom itself has had its largest impact there. But tighter financial 
conditions—both in equities and rates—are the most obvious channel through which the 
economic impact could also increase.

### Page 23

A Gradual Shift from Rate Risk to Growth Risk
Our inflation and policy views remain more distinctive than our growth views. With our
central bank views generally more dovish than the markets, particularly as we move
deeper into 2027, our baseline forecast implies scope for front-end pricing to fall
further. The mix of high fiscal deficits and the ongoing AI capex boom limit the scope for
longer-dated yields to fall in our baseline. But if policy rates peak at the lower levels we

expect, forward rates should also move lower (we see the US 10-year yield at 4.6% at
the end of 2027). Faster energy relief would accentuate that process.
This kind of policy relief would be helpful for both equities and bonds. But shifts in the
nature of the risks facing asset markets may be starting to alter the balance of risks
between equities and bonds, and correlations across asset markets, as we move into
2027. Through much of 2026, global bonds failed to protect equity portfolios. The

inflationary impulse from energy disruptions and the surprising resilience of growth and
labor markets in the face of those shocks prompted a sharp shift in policy rate
expectations. The start of a broad-based G10 hiking cycle has pushed real rates sharply
higher in the last 9 months. Under the surface, the mix of rising corporate sector
financing needs with still-high government deficits helped to underpin the upward
pressure on global real yields.

In the near term, the key risks to our baseline economic forecasts still push in that
direction. The most obvious is that any escalation in the Iran war could push energy
prices sharply higher again in the coming months. Particularly after the fall in the risk
premium in oil markets coming into November, a fresh energy shock would hurt equities,
support the US dollar and put renewed upward pressure on rates markets. Recent
firmness in core inflation, and Fed commentary, are also likely to keep worries about a

more front-loaded hiking cycle alive. The impulse from AI investment is also arguably
moving through its strongest point, which may limit any decline in yields.
But the further we move into 2027, the more likely it is that the key risks are positive for
bonds, as the threats from central bank hikes, energy price spikes and the AI investment
tailwind recede and other risks become more prominent. A sharper reversal in the AI
boom or earlier AI-related labor market disruption are the most obvious versions of that

story. But the kinds of financial stresses from a high-rate environment that we saw
emerge in September and October—most obviously in France heading towards the April
election but potentially also elsewhere—would push in a similar direction, as would
“black swan” risks such as a major cybersecurity event.
12 November 2026   23
Goldman Sachs Global Economics Analyst

### Page 24

Diversification and Protection as the Market Cycle Matures 
With a steady economic backdrop, our baseline forecasts for 2027 are for positive 
returns in equities and bonds, and close to zero returns in commodities. But with a 
maturing market cycle and significant uncertainty around core drivers of the outlook, we 
continue to emphasize the value of diversification and hedges to protect against key 
risks.

We expect ongoing earnings growth alongside the recent valuation compression to 
support further gains in major equity markets in 2027. As the pace of earnings growth 
slows moving into 2027, the pace of US equity gains is likely to drop too, moving well 
below the more than 20% annualized rate of the last three years. With earnings growth

### Page 25

likely to be slower still in 2028, the risk is that the market pulls forward its worries about
a deeper turn in the cycle. Accounting for risks around the baseline view, we recommend
an overweight allocation in the US and Japan, neutral Asia ex Japan/EM, and
underweight in Europe.
We expect further widening in credit spreads in 2027 as the increase in corporate supply
continues. Although widening is modest in our base case, risks are clearly skewed in that

direction. It is hard to see scenarios where US credit spreads tighten sharply (there is
more scope for tightening in Europe where the risk premium has risen more), and easier
to see how spreads could move higher, particularly if economic conditions deteriorate
more than we expect or if investors become more uncomfortable about the profitability
of the AI investment boom. We expect US HY to underperform, with some risk that
problems in the tail of weaker borrowers create more turbulent conditions for broader

financing. Without the large capital inflows and rate cuts prompted by the EM crises in
1997-98, the sustained divergence between credit and equity markets from 1998 to
2000 may more challenging now.
Government bonds offer better returns than in recent years, reflecting higher yields and
improving relative valuations. Real bond yields are at high levels compared with the last
30 years, both absolutely and relative to equities.  Our baseline is for bonds to deliver a

little more than the decent running yield that they currently offer, though the scope for
capital gains is likely to be higher later in 2027. There are risks to that view from fresh
energy shocks, or stickier core inflation than we expect. But if clearer downside risks to
growth emerge, yields could fall more clearly.
After tight ranges in 2026, our baseline is for modest USD weakness as lower inflation
and energy put the USD on the back foot, alongside further appreciation in CNY

underpinned by the surging trade surplus. But if the market challenges US tech
exceptionalism more quickly, we could see a sharper shift away from the USD. And the
same set of growth risks that are increasing the hedging value of bonds would also likely
prompt a sharper appreciation in the Japanese Yen.
Commodities continue to offer value as a portfolio hedge. Despite the ongoing war with
Iran, oil market supply has largely been restored, but risks of escalation and further

disruptions have led to a large but variable risk premium above the oil price justified by
the fundamental supply-demand balance. Our baseline is that this risk premium will
slowly erode, leaving energy and core commodity returns more muted. But there is still
heightened risk of scenarios where energy prices could move much higher, hurting
bonds and equities. While there is also a downside tail to energy prices, if there is faster
progress towards a resolution of the war with Iran, in that scenario equities and bonds

would likely be performing well.
12 November 2026   25
Goldman Sachs Global Economics Analyst

### Page 26

Alongside diversification, direct hedges are worth considering. Positioning for lower 
rates in the US and Japan, or for jointly lower rates and equities are potential ways to 
protect against deeper downside growth tails. In FX, USD/JPY downside may play a 
similar role, given that it generally performs well when rates and equities are both falling. 
For energy upside, hedges in European gas futures and TTF calls look like asymmetric 
ways to protect against risk. So far, the sharpest volatility has been under the surface of 
the major equity indices. Equity index volatility has risen a little but is unlikely to fall 
much from here and we think has scope to rise further if macro stresses broaden. 
Deeper protection in equities themselves—either through out-of-the-money puts or 
replacing equity holdings with longer-dated call options—also seems more valuable 
than usual as the cycle matures.

and desk-top solutions.

PROPRIETARY RESEARCH FROM LSEG October 30, 2026
WEEKLY AGGREGATES
ITEM DEFINITIONS AND METHODOLOGY
3© 2026 LSEG.  All marks herein are used under licence. TRPR_82222_875
General:
All aggregates are bottom-up aggregates calculated from analysts' consensus forecasts unless otherwise noted. Bottom-up
aggregates are weighted calculations using the I/B/E/S data from LSEG share class monitored or the official index weight of
the index-provider. Top-down forecasts are compilations of strategists' estimates. I/B/E/S universe calculations are based on

all companies within a given market monitored by LSEG.
Benchmark index calculations, including those for official local market indices, the S&P 500 Index, and the FTSE World Equity
Indices are calculated using official index constituents and weightings. All index composites are representative of current
index constituents. Index constituents and weightings are updated monthly during options expiration (the Thursday before the
third Friday of every month) for every index except the S&P 500, the constituents of which are current as of the date of

calculation.
PEIGBY refers to the Price Earnings Inverse Government Bond Yield ratio, which measures equity valuation with respect to
the inverse-yield on long-term government bonds (now known as the 'Fed' model). All PEIGBY ratios are I/B/E/S universe-
specific except for the US ratio, where it is based on the S&P 500 Index.
Methodology:
For US and selected Canadian aggregates, calendar-year aggregation is performed using the sum of four quarters' estimates

and a quarterly pro-ration is applied to non-December year end companies' quarterly EPS estimates. Outside of the US and
Canada, all company-level data are calendarized prior to aggregation using the Compustat method of calendarization.
Aggregation is performed using the annual consensus EPS estimate and a May roll-back is applied to December year-end
markets. Aggregates for Japan, where most companies report in March, use an August rollback and a November rollback is

used for Australia and New Zealand, where the fiscal year-end for most companies is in June.
All 12-month-forward data for ex-US markets are calculated using a pro-rata weighting to annual EPS forecasts. The
weightings applied are a daily calibration out of 365 days relative to each constituent's individual fiscal year-end and the
current date.
All non-surprise data, including aggregate forecast earnings, forecast growth, and forward-looking valuation calculations are

weighted according to the I/B/E/S share class monitored for I/B/E/S universes or the weightings assigned to each constituent
in a given index. Aggregated earnings calculations are the equivalent of net profit aggregates, as they are derived from the
EPS forecasts aggregated to the net income level by the appropriate share weighting.
Earnings Aggregation:
For 12-month-forward earnings, the 12-month-forward EPS is used in place of the calendarized EPS in the formulas below.

I/B/E/S universe EPS Aggregate = Index EPS Aggregate =
Growth Calculations:
All growth calculations are the weighted year over year growth in earnings for the calendarized fiscal year period. The
formulas below use index shares for index growth calculations.
Calendarized Earnings Growth = 12-month-forward Growth =
Valuation:
All valuation calculations are the weighted-average price-earnings ratio for the fiscal period in question. The formulas below

use index shares for index P/E calculations.
Calendarized P/E Aggregate = 12-month-forward P/E =
Revisions Momentum:
Unless otherwise indicated, analysts' revisions momentum is a simple aggregation of the total number of estimates raised
divided by the total number of estimates lowered. Thus, any revisions ratio less than 1 indicates negative revisions
momentum while a ratio greater than 1 indicates positive revisions momentum, with 1 marking parity.

The Global Aggregates from LSEG product contains annual aggregates for universes included in this publication, including
supplemental aggregate data of over 100 items as well as history dating back to 1985 for North America and to 1987 for other
markets. To learn more about this product, contact your LSEG sales representative.
FTSE and the FTSE World Equity Indices are owned by FTSE International Limited.

Rates are back under upward pressure, reviving our tactical
bearish view. AI risks have become more idiosyncratic, but
upcoming catalysts could quickly reverse the move. France
remains under pressure, while Italy could see a rating upgrade

Michiel Tukker and Benjamin Schroeder
Tactically bearish on rates but AI event risk
ahead of us
Price action suggests global rates are happy to push higher again,
and we therefore return to our tactical bearish call. Risk sentiment is
improving, with AI worries turning more idiosyncratic, focused
mostly on NVIDIA’s leadership questions. Amid more stable risk
conditions, but without strong catalysts, the 2yr USD swap jumped
up by some 12bp on the day. Of course, one could argue oil ticked
higher and jobless numbers were robust, but nothing that would
warrant such a strong reaction by itself.
Buying the dip seems to be the recurring theme in equity markets,
but we warn of some key event risks ahead. Next week, we have

### Page 2

both Anthropic’s IPO pricing and NVIDIA announcing third-quarter
results. These two events are seen as important bellwethers for the
broader future of AI and can also shake the US macro outlook. From
the past weeks, we’ve seen how any disappointments can quickly
push US rates lower.
Europe is relatively more shielded from AI, but global risk sentiment
can be quick to pull down longer rates in particular. We argue that
the front end of the EUR and GBP curves should remain focused on

inﬂation, but the belly of the curve is more exposed to sentiment.
The euro swap curve still paints a very optimistic view of the ECB’s
terminal rate above 3%, which in our eyes can only hold if the
growth outlook improves materially. A global setback in AI would
push against this narrative, raising expectations for rate cuts after
the initial hiking cycle.
EGBs drift with the broader sentiment while
Italy is up for review by S&P
The mood in the European bond market has again darkened

somewhat. In part, this owed to the spillover from tech-related
equity jitters and unease around geopolitics after the latest
comments from US President Donald Trump, but the general
repricing higher of rates, which originated in the US, has done its
share as well.
France has lagged its peers with the 10y OAT/Bund back towards
150bp. There has been little news on the situation in France, but it is
worth noting that any hopes of ECB President Christine Lagarde

pulling France out of its political deadlock were dampened after the
ﬁrst polls this week showed she would get only 14% of the ﬁrst-
round votes, similar to other centrist candidates. This would still see
election frontrunner Marine Le Pen and left-wing rival Jean-Luc
Mélenchon advance to the second round, leaving one of the tail risk
scenarios on the table.
Italy, meanwhile, will face a scheduled review date by S&P, which
currently rates Italy BBB+ with a positive outlook. That outlook has

been in place since January, which would make a ﬁrst rating
upgrade back to single-A a possibility. Considering the more

Friday's events and market view
The European session will see a busy slate of ECB speakers,
including Isabel Schnabel. Data comes in the form of ﬁnal
October inﬂation readings for France and Spain, as well as
the second reading for Q3 eurozone GDP growth. The US will
watch the October PPIs and a continued busy slate of Fed
speakers, including Susan Collins, Lorie Logan and Michelle
Bowman. After markets close, eyes will turn to the scheduled
rating reviews. The main focus is Italy, rated BBB+/Positive by

S&P. Fitch has slated a possible review of Germany
(AAA/Stable), and DBRS has slated Portugal (AH/Positive) and
the UK (AA/Stable).

重點摘要
• 雲端巨頭資本支出持平，伺
服器仍優於消費終端
《雲端巨頭資本支出持平，伺服器仍優於消費終端》
3Q26財報顯示，谷哥、亞馬遜、微軟與Meta在
2026年資本支出目標合計達7,300億美元，較上季
略減25億美元(-0.3%)，減少主要是微軟外部第三方
算力產能延遲導致資本支出目標下修50億美元，與
Meta上調25億美元相互抵消。雖然雲端巨頭支出
持平但資料中心晶片需求仍強勁。英特爾3Q26財報
顯示，受益於資料中心與AI事業帶動營收達165億
美元、年增21%。其中資料中心與人工智慧相關營
收73億美元續創歷史新高、年增78%，2027年相關
晶片仍將供不應求。反觀個人電腦營收81億美元、
年減5%。英特爾預估2026年個人電腦出貨量將年

減12%、2027年受到記憶體與儲存供給影響預估將
續減10%。
受到上半年消費者預期終端售價上漲提前換機，
3Q26個人電腦與智慧手機出貨均呈現大幅下滑。根
據IDC統計，3Q26全球個人電腦出貨量達6,270萬
台，較2025年同期衰退20%。同樣受到記憶體漲價
與供應短缺衝擊的智慧手機3Q26出貨量2.6億台，
較2025年同期衰退22%。由於未來記憶體與儲存價
格小幅調漲以及供給受限下，消費終端價格仍將維
持高價位。因此，IDC預估個人電腦與智慧手機
2027年將高機率維持衰退，預估要2028年出貨量
才有望復甦。
資料來源：公司，2026/10/30，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/30

吸納量
重點摘要
3
《類比晶片終端需求上升，工業、資料中心占比較
高供應商營收更具成長性》
德州儀器(TXN)3Q26營收60.6億美元、EPS 2.62美
元雙雙優於預期，4Q26財測營收58-63億美元，中
位數較市場普遍預期高出6%，反映資料中心市場
需求持續成長，使營收預測優於往年季節性回落。
以3Q26終端市場來看，工業營收年增超過40%、
季增約10%，成長來自航太國防、能源基礎設施等
領域；車用營收年增約15%、季增約10%，受益於
客戶庫存水準降低及電動車占比提高；資料中心營
收則年增約95%、季增20%，為成長最快的終端市
場。在營運方面，產能利用率提高以及2027年潛在
的價格調漲可能進一步擴大獲利成長空間，儘管資

本支出可能高於2026年的20~30億美元，但顯示終
端需求正擴大成長。
恩智浦(NXPI)3Q26營收37.9億美元、EPS 4.20美元
優於預期，但4Q26財測營收中位數38.5億美元略
低於市場預期。以3Q26終端市場來看，車用營收
20.7億美元、年增13%，成長主要來自軟體定義汽
車(SDV)產品貢獻；工業與物聯網營收8.02億美元、
年增39%，主要由資料中心、工廠自動化和醫療保
健等產業帶動；通訊基礎設施營收5.03億美元、年
增54%，得益於資料中心業務成長。恩智浦財報結
果顯示各終端市場需求持續上升，惟成長速度較慢
的車用營收占比超過五成，未來成長速度可能不及
工業和資料中心營收占比更高的同業。

4
《券商 CIO 調查顯示軟體支出預期持續增溫》
券商 CIO 調查指出 2027 年的企業 IT 採購預算仍維
持成長。CIO 現在預計 2026 年的 IT 採購預算將年
增 4.0%，自 2Q26 走高 20bps，連續 3 次增溫。
對於 2027 年 IT 採購預算的首次估計，CIO 預期年
增 4.0%，雖僅與更新後的 2026 年預期相符，但實
則是近 4 年來的開年新高。在這之中，軟體預算成
長優於領先硬體、網通與服務，預期年增 4.4%，且

自 2026 年加速 10bps。2027 年軟體支出的預期加
速可能進一步說明短期內 AI 並未帶來全面性衝擊。
資料來源：摩根史丹利，2026/10/12，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/20
人工智慧與機器學習 (AI/ML) 的支出優先性仍穩居
CIO 調查的第一名，佔比雖較前幾期調研回落，但
這應該是因為 CIO 對於開始增加其他項目的優先性。
AI 相關專案的預算仍最不可能被刪除，且淨占比
(即最不可能被刪除 – 最可能被刪除的比例)，3Q26
較 2Q26 大幅走揚 13ppts 至 17%。網路安全的支
出優先性則連 4 季走揚，且最不可能被刪除的淨占
比也走揚 1ppt 至 16%。持續反映出 AI 帶動的攻擊
面擴大、攻擊手法更加多元，與攻擊成本大幅走低，
導致企業在 AI 顯著產生正面效益前，優先選擇防範
未然的心態。而隨著 AI 的能力仍在快速發展，如近
期 Anthropic 研究即指出 AI 已可自我迭代，且小
型模型未來可能具備在物聯網裝置上自我複製的能

力，本行預期網安的需求可望仍持續強健。數位轉
型雖仍處第 3，但在本次券商調查中大幅地低於過
硬體 軟體 通訊設備 IT服務

Document 546: 中东原油“全面夺回”亚洲市场，美国至亚洲油轮运价从高点跌61%

Source PDF: all report pdf/中东原油“全面夺回”亚洲市场，美国至亚洲油轮运价从高点跌61%.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

中东原油对亚洲出⼝回暖，亚洲炼油商采购重⼼转回传统供应来源，美国原油替代需求减
弱。美国墨西哥湾⾄亚洲 VLCC ⽇收益较 10 ⽉峰值跌 61% ，波斯湾⾄东亚仅跌 24% 。⽇
本、泰国等重启或扩⼤中东采购， 11 ⽉交付美国原油订单骤降约 50% 。
摘要：
中东原油流向亚洲正在回升，亚洲炼油商的采购重⼼逐步转回传统供应来源，美国原油的替代
性需求随之减弱。
11 ⽉ 13 ⽇，据彭博报道，美国墨西哥湾⾄亚洲航线的超⼤型原油运输船（ VLCC ）⽇收益较 10
⽉峰值下跌 61% ，⽽波斯湾⾄东亚航线的同类指标仅下跌 24% ，两条航线运价⾛势明显分化。
采购数据也印证了这⼀趋势。⽇本、泰国等亚洲炼油商正在重启或扩⼤中东原油采购。彭博此
前报道称，亚洲炼油商订购的 11 ⽉交付美国原油数量较此前骤降约 50% ，⾄ 2400 万⾄ 2600 万
桶。
据报道援引知情交易员，来⾃阿联酋、沙特阿拉伯和卡塔尔的原油流量均已显著扩⼤，其中包
括需要穿越霍尔⽊兹海峡运输的货物。随着中东产油国产量恢复、售价下调，亚洲原油进⼝格

局正逐步回归美伊冲突前的状态。
中东供应恢复，产油国下调售价争夺市场
今年 2 ⽉美伊冲突爆发初期，途经霍尔⽊兹海峡的油轮遭到袭击，波斯湾产油国原油出⼝⼤幅收
缩，亚洲买家被迫转向美国等替代供应来源。
随着局势趋于稳定，中东原油供应逐步恢复。沙特阿拉伯向欧佩克报告称，其 10 ⽉原油产量升
⾄近年来的⽉度⾼位之⼀。
与此同时，主要产油国也在通过降价争夺亚洲市场份额。沙特近期将⾯向亚洲买家的官⽅售价
下调⾄六年来低位；已于 4 ⽉退出欧佩克的阿联酋，也下调了 12 ⽉交付旗舰原油品种的价格溢
价。
荷兰国际集团（ ING Groep NV ）⼤宗商品策略主管 Warren Patterson 表⽰，亚洲很难摆脱对中
东这⼀最⼤原油供应来源的依赖，因为全球供需平衡中缺乏⾜够的额外供应，难以⽀持采购来
源的⼤规模转移。
亚洲买家回流中东，美国原油订单减少
⽇本炼油商已经开始增加中东原油采购。 Eneos Holdings 和 Cosmo Energy Holdings 近期均有
采购阿联酋原油的记录。

乐天证券经济研究所分析师 Yutaro Nishi 表⽰，只要⽇本炼油商仍能从中东获得原油，就很可能
继续采购。他指出，尽管各国情况有所不同，但亚洲仍将是中东原油的主要市场。
印度的采购结构也出现变化。根据 Kpler 数据，来⾃波斯湾的原油进⼝量已超过俄罗斯，重新成
为印度最⼤的原油进⼝来源。

10 ⽉份，社会消费品零售总额 46463 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 0.4% 。其中，除汽⻋以外的消费品零

品零售额 56546 亿元，增⻓ 2.2% 。 10 ⽉份，城镇消费品零售额 40098 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 0.2% ；乡
